● OMI.Polytope now classifies shapes by degree signature, as your Shape
  Signature rule says, without naming them. I took the rule from that older
  project, not its files. Everything is pushed: omi-types, boxedPINE's types
  pointer, and unbound 4e6f4c4. All three of omi-types' required checks pass.

  What it does. It reads each shape's signature from how its vertices, edges and
  faces connect:
  - how many edges meet at each vertex;
  - how many edges bound each face.

  When every face has the same p and every vertex the same q, the result is
  Uniform {p, q}; otherwise it's Mixed.

  Results:

  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Shape      │               Signature               │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tetrahedron    │ {3, 3}                                │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cube           │ {4, 3}                                │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Octahedron     │ {3, 4}                                │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Torus grid     │ {4, 4}, uniform but not a sphere      │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Square pyramid │ mixed (apex degree 4, base corners 3) │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  - {p, q} is now computed from structure for the three solids, like their Betti
    numbers, not taken from what a table declares.
  - The torus shows a uniform signature isn't enough on its own. The Platonic
    class is a uniform signature together with Betti numbers (1, 0, 1).

  Next are your Archimedean transforms, truncation and snub, and their Catalan
  duals, built on this signature.

● Here's the plan:
  - Core classification. OMI.Polytope gets two checks over bits, alongside the
    degree signature:
    - archimedean: every vertex has the same multiset of face sizes around it.
      That's the vertex configuration, for example 3.6.6.
    - catalan: every face has the same multiset of vertex degrees. That's the
      dual condition.
  - Transforms in the tests. Truncation, rectification and the dual are built
    from each solid's face cycles. Each result is then measured: counts,
    signature, Betti numbers and both classes.

  This also settles the template's two wrong rows by construction: truncating
  the tetrahedron and rectifying the cube will produce their real counts.

  incidence (so degree signatures and Betti numbers can't distinguish them), the

● Bash(cd /home/main/Programs/unbound && cat >> development.vtt <<'E'…)
  ⎿  4820f9b development.vtt: the endianness probe is the snub's chirality

● I've recorded this as the source of the snub's chirality, cue
  chirality-source, pushed as unbound 4820f9b.

  The endianness probe makes byte order a handedness:
  - BOM FEFF, big-endian: the right-handed snub.
  - BOM FFFE, little-endian: the left-handed snub.
  - The 8th bit, parity: M, the mirror.

  Both programs run unchanged. This machine is little-endian, so the probe
  reports BOM FFFE, the left-handed snub.

  There's also a prediction for when snubbing is built. A snub's two hands are
  mirror images with exactly the same incidence. So counts, degree signature and
  Betti numbers will all come out identical for both, and that's correct, not a
  failure. The hand is only in orientation: the cyclic order of faces around
  each vertex. That's where your probe puts byte order, which swap16 normalizes.
  So the snub needs an orientation added to OMI.Polytope, not only incidence,
  and the check is that the two hands differ there and nowhere else.

  ⎿  e60656f development.vtt: the Universal Codeword, the author's partitio

● I've recorded it as cue universal-codeword, pushed as unbound e60656f.

  Your thinking then. Everything grows from one unit, NUL, the axis:
  - Characters by count: the pair NULNUL earns each control character by how
    many NULs there are.
  - Frames and registers: then come the frame NUL SOH ~ DEL and the 8-character
    register.
  - The theorem: from 8 preheaders, NUL^k SOH NUL^(7-k), the delta law reaches
    all 64 code points in 8 orbits, with the constant choosing the chirality.

  That is partitions and blocks: a register cut into positions, and one orbit
  per position. The WordNet path fits it. wordnet-to-geometry.c reads WordNet's
  synsets, glosses and hypernyms, finds each synset's depth from the roots, and
  maps depth and part of speech to Aegean geometry.

  What's checked and what isn't. The theorem is kept as you wrote it, a proof
  sketch, unchecked. Next to it the cue records what zak measured: the period is
  8 at 16 bits and half the width at any power of two. On a register of eight
  bytes (64 bits) the period is 32. So the sketch's "period 8 on the 8-element
  register" holds when each element is 2 bits.

  Its claim that 8 preheaders reach all 64 code points can be checked directly
  in zak. Want me to run that check?

  ⎿  d93f683 development.vtt: XOR learned from the Polybius diagonal, cited
      to its proofs
● I've recorded it with its proofs, cue polybius-xor, pushed as unbound d93f683.

  Put the nibbles 0 to 15 in a 4×4 Polybius square:
  - The main diagonal is 0, 5, 10, 15. It XORs to 0 and sums to 30 (0x1E).
  - The anti-diagonal is 3, 6, 9, 12. It also XORs to 0 and sums to 30.
  - Together the two diagonals sum to 60 (0x3C).

  It's proved in the locked Coq registry, in
  proofs/coq/02-closure/DiagonalGaugeCloses.v. Among its theorems are
  dplus_xor_zero, dminus_xor_zero, diag_sum_3c and
  dplus_is_polybius_main_diagonal. The same theorems are in the π proof from
  omi-tetragrammatron, and core.test.ts checks them too. So the operation you
  learned XOR from is now one of the registry's proved closures.

● I found the glTF models and recorded them as gltf (unbound a14569d).
  - The conformance set. 482 glTF and GLB models, most in light-garden's
    GeneratedAssets-0.6.1. Its README confirms what the split means: the
    Positive models are valid per the glTF specification, the Negative ones are
    invalid. So it's a ready golden and must-reject set for glTF, the same
    discipline as every check here.
  - In the Commons. boxedPINE loads the shiba model (shiba.glb) in four modules.
    icon.ts renders a model into four canvases, which makes a model an agent's
    icon.

  That makes your final check concrete. If Solomon's icon is a glTF model,
  changing his icon means changing his model: the two states differ there (XOR).
  His story stays the same (XNOR). And the trace can confirm the change shows
  only where his icon is.

● I've recorded this as replay-video and linked it to the acceptance test in the
  ledger (unbound b0e0b8d). The final check now has its form:

  1. Two canvases, each transferred to a worker with
     transferControlToOffscreen().
  2. One story, from the same states, with Solomon's icon, a glTF model, being
     the only difference.
  3. Each canvas becomes a video through captureStream(), recorded so it can be
     replayed exactly.
  4. The narrative track runs on both as the shared clock.
  5. Compare frame by frame: the frames must differ only where the icon is (XOR)
     and match everywhere else (XNOR).

  That's the next thing to build: a page you can watch, with two videos side by
  side, played and replayed, judged by eye.
